People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yes… yes, I have. More than I like to admit. I used to keep up with things at church, little responsibilities, nothing grand, but people counted on me. But lately I don’t feel safe being in there by myself. My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, helping cover things or telling me not to go out there alone. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.

I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. Everything feels far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don't seem to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like seeing a person standing there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll think I heard my wife call from the other room, but then it’s just the pipes or the refrigerator kicking on—you know how old houses make their own little noises, ours has done that since we moved in. But no, I haven’t really noticed things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing help with everyday tasks that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. More than I like to admit. I used to keep up with things at church, little responsibilities, nothing grand, but people counted on me. And the garden too—my tomatoes, I used to be very particular about the rows, my father taught me that when I was a boy—but now it’s just gotten away from me.\n\nAnd the youth maker program… that one hurts. I was always comfortable in the workshop, showing the kids how to measure twice, keep their hands clear, all that. But lately I don’t feel safe being in there by myself. My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, helping cover things or telling me not to go out there alone. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Everything feels far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don't seem to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like seeing a person standing there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll think I heard my wife call from the other room, but then it’s just the pipes or the refrigerator kicking on—you know how old houses make their own little noises, ours has done that since we moved in. But no, I haven’t really noticed things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing help with everyday tasks that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. More than I like to admit. I used to keep up with things at church, little responsibilities, nothing grand, but people counted on me. And the garden too—my tomatoes, I used to be very particular about the rows, my father taught me that when I was a boy—but now it’s just gotten away from me.\n\nAnd the youth maker program… that one hurts. I was always comfortable in the workshop, showing the kids how to measure twice, keep their hands clear, all that. But lately I don’t feel safe being in there by myself. My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, helping cover things or telling me not to go out there alone. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that you are somehow to blame for things, or that you are a burden or not much use?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel that way. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. And then—well, I spend time out near the workshop, or I used to, anyway, with all the tools and the little projects piled up… I start getting scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone there too long.\n\nI don’t like saying that. I know that wasn’t exactly what you asked, or maybe it was. But yes, I feel like a burden.\n\nDoctor: When your mood briefly feels a little lighter, does your memory get better?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if it gets better just because my mood lifts for a minute. Maybe a little, maybe not.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I want to say it’s—see, this is exactly the kind of thing that’s been happening.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I don’t know if I believe it the way you’d believe something sensible, but the thought keeps coming up that they’d be better off without me.\n\nAnd when I’m out near the workshop—there are all those tools, old projects I never finished, things from when I used to keep busy—I get frightened of what I might start thinking if I stayed there alone too long. So yes… that thought is there, and it scares me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sort of feeling right here in my chest, like something’s missing but also weighing me down, if that makes sense. And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. I was always comfortable in the workshop, showing the kids how to measure twice, keep their hands clear, all that. But lately I don’t feel safe being in there by myself. My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, helping cover things or telling me not to go out there alone. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sort of feeling right here in my chest, like something’s missing but also weighing me down, if that makes sense. And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that you are somehow to blame for things, or that you are a burden or not much use?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel that way. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I don’t think so… not like suddenly looking around and not knowing where I am.

I mean, I can walk into the garage and forget why I went in there, and then I notice the old paint cans still need sorting, and then I start thinking about whether I ever labeled the shelves properly—sorry, that’s not exactly it.

I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. Sometimes a word sits there just out of reach, like I’ll point at the… you know, the thing on the counter, but I think everybody does that some.

I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—you know, the kind of thing I used to do without even thinking, getting the words down, a few numbers, dates, whatever—and now it’s like they just scatter on me. I stare at the page and it all slips away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, most days, honestly. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sort of feeling right here in my chest, like something’s missing but also weighing me down, if that makes sense. And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. Everything feels far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don't seem to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like seeing a person standing there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll think I heard my wife call from the other room, but then it’s just the pipes or the refrigerator kicking on—you know how old houses make their own little noises, ours has done that since we moved in. But no, I haven’t really noticed things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing help with everyday tasks that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. More than I like to admit. I used to keep up with things at church, little responsibilities, nothing grand, but people counted on me. And the garden too—my tomatoes, I used to be very particular about the rows, my father taught me that when I was a boy—but now it’s just gotten away from me.\n\nAnd the youth maker program… that one hurts. I was always comfortable in the workshop, showing the kids how to measure twice, keep their hands clear, all that. But lately I don’t feel safe being in there by myself. My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, helping cover things or telling me not to go out there alone. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that you are somehow to blame for things, or that you are a burden or not much use?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel that way. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. And then—well, I spend time out near the workshop, or I used to, anyway, with all the tools and the little projects piled up… I start getting scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone there too long.\n\nI don’t like saying that. I know that wasn’t exactly what you asked, or maybe it was. But yes, I feel like a burden.\n\nDoctor: When your mood briefly feels a little lighter, does your memory get better?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if it gets better just because my mood lifts for a minute. Maybe a little, maybe not.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I want to say it’s—see, this is exactly the kind of thing that’s been happening.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I don’t know if I believe it the way you’d believe something sensible, but the thought keeps coming up that they’d be better off without me.\n\nAnd when I’m out near the workshop—there are all those tools, old projects I never finished, things from when I used to keep busy—I get frightened of what I might start thinking if I stayed there alone too long. So yes… that thought is there, and it scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel certain that you have ruined things for your family, even when others tell you that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say certain. That sounds so… fixed, like a stamp on a document.\n\nI do worry I’ve made everything harder for them. I turn that over and over, and then someone will say, “No, that’s not true,” and for a little while I try to believe them. But it doesn’t quite stay put.\n\nIt’s like when I was sorting old papers in the garage—no, that’s not really what you asked. I’m sorry. I just mean… I’m not sure I can trust my own judgment about it. I don’t know if I’ve ruined things. I’m afraid I have.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood felt okay for several days, but your memory was still this bad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. The memory part—or thinking part, I suppose—has just been bad enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do in my sleep, and then the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll look at the page like… what was I trying to say?\n\nWhether my mood was okay for several days at the same time, I don’t know. I get caught on the fact that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I haven’t even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, and that’s usually the sort of thing I’d drift toward without thinking.\n\nI was supposed to help with choir planning too, and chess—well, I’ve just skipped it. And fishing, even that sounds like… more work than it’s worth, which is strange for me. I used to think about the tackle box, the little repairs, all of that. Now it just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever forget where you are, even in a place you know well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like suddenly looking around and not knowing where I am.\n\nI mean, I can walk into the garage and forget why I went in there, and then I notice the old paint cans still need sorting, and then I start thinking about whether I ever labeled the shelves properly—sorry, that’s not exactly it.\n\nIn places I know well, I don’t think I’ve forgotten where I am. It’s more that I lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Before you started feeling depressed, were your memory problems already there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know where to draw the line with “before,” honestly. What I can say is, the reason I came in is that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to do without even thinking—and then the numbers and words just… scatter. I end up staring at it, and it’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to do it. Whether that started first or the low mood did, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If someone gives you a hint, does it usually help you remember what you were trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes a hint helps a little, but not reliably. The reason I came in is more that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do in my sleep—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Someone can say, “You were going to mention the schedule,” and I’ll think, right, the schedule… but then I’m already losing the next piece of it. It’s like trying to keep sawdust in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble naming familiar objects or people?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. Sometimes a word sits there just out of reach, like I’ll point at the… you know, the thing on the counter, but I think everybody does that some.\n\nPeople’s names—well, I’ve never been great with names. At the hardware store I used to know half the fellows by sight, but names would float off unless I’d seen them a dozen times. That’s always been sort of true.\n\nSo, familiar objects or people? I haven’t really noticed that as a big problem. Not like looking at a chair and not knowing it’s a chair.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Whether that started first or the low mood did, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If someone gives you a hint, does it usually help you remember what you were trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes a hint helps a little, but not reliably. The reason I came in is more that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do in my sleep—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Someone can say, “You were going to mention the schedule,” and I’ll think, right, the schedule… but then I’m already losing the next piece of it. It’s like trying to keep sawdust in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble naming familiar objects or people?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. Sometimes a word sits there just out of reach, like I’ll point at the… you know, the thing on the counter, but I think everybody does that some.\n\nPeople’s names—well, I’ve never been great with names. At the hardware store I used to know half the fellows by sight, but names would float off unless I’d seen them a dozen times. That’s always been sort of true.\n\nSo, familiar objects or people? I haven’t really noticed that as a big problem. Not like looking at a chair and not knowing it’s a chair.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the month, do you often lose track of what day it is?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore—I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could do in my sleep before, and the numbers and words scatter on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The day, yeah… I lose the thread of it sometimes, but what really got me here is that my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.

I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a plain little note, nothing complicated—and before, I could do that practically in my sleep. Now the numbers and the words scatter on me, and I end up sitting there feeling foolish. The day of the week sort of gets mixed into that, I guess, but it’s more like everything slips away when I try to hold it still.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—you know, the kind of thing I used to do without even thinking, getting the words down, a few numbers, dates, whatever—and now it’s like they just scatter on me. I stare at the page and it all slips away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, most days, honestly. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sort of feeling right here in my chest, like something’s missing but also weighing me down, if that makes sense. And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. Everything feels far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don't seem to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like seeing a person standing there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that you are somehow to blame for things, or that you are a burden or not much use?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel that way. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. But yes, I feel like a burden.\n\nDoctor: When your mood briefly feels a little lighter, does your memory get better?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if it gets better just because my mood lifts for a minute. Maybe a little, maybe not.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I want to say it’s—see, this is exactly the kind of thing that’s been happening.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I don’t know if I believe it the way you’d believe something sensible, but the thought keeps coming up that they’d be better off without me.\n\nAnd when I’m out near the workshop—there are all those tools, old projects I never finished, things from when I used to keep busy—I get frightened of what I might start thinking if I stayed there alone too long. The bills… my wife has been doing more of that lately. I used to keep a pretty good handle on things, or at least I thought I did.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—you know, the kind of thing I used to do without even thinking, getting the words down, a few numbers, dates, whatever—and now it’s like they just scatter on me. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sort of feeling right here in my chest, like something’s missing but also weighing me down, if that makes sense. And my wife will be talking to me kindly—she’s a good woman, always has been, even back when we were first setting up the house and she’d label every little cupboard—and I hear her, I know she’s being gentle, but I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. Everything feels far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don't seem to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not like seeing a person standing there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean. It makes me feel… well, it makes me feel like I’m not much use.\n\nDoctor: Have your memory problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a little reminder, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I’ll know what I meant to say, then it slips off somewhere. It’s embarrassing… I used to be the one people asked to keep things straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d say getting lost, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll go into a store and forget what aisle I meant to go down, but that’s not the same thing, is it? My wife always says those big grocery stores move everything around anyway, and they do—used to be you could count on where the coffee was, now it’s all “seasonal displays” and nonsense.\n\nBut places I should know… I don’t think I’ve been truly lost like that. Not that I can recall right now.\n\nDoctor: Did your memory trouble begin after a stroke?\n\nPatient: A stroke? No… I don’t think so. Nobody ever told me I’d had one.\n\nI mean, my father had trouble with his blood pressure, and after church folks get to talking about who had what scan or who’s on which pill, and it all runs together in my head sometimes. But as far as me, I don’t recall any stroke happening before this memory trouble started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that you are somehow to blame for things, or that you are a burden or not much use?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel that way. More than I want to admit.\n\nI keep thinking my family might be better off without me, like I’m just one more thing they have to worry about. Maybe a little, maybe not.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I want to say it’s—see, this is exactly the kind of thing that’s been happening.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I don’t know if I believe it the way you’d believe something sensible, but the thought keeps coming up that they’d be better off without me.\n\nAnd when I’m out near the workshop—there are all those tools, old projects I never finished, things from when I used to keep busy—I get frightened of what I might start thinking if I stayed there alone too long. So yes… that thought is there, and it scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel certain that you have ruined things for your family, even when others tell you that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say certain. That sounds so… fixed, like a stamp on a document.\n\nI do worry I’ve made everything harder for them. I turn that over and over, and then someone will say, “No, that’s not true,” and for a little while I try to believe them. But it doesn’t quite stay put.\n\nIt’s like when I was sorting old papers in the garage—no, that’s not really what you asked. I’m sorry. I just mean… I’m not sure I can trust my own judgment about it. I don’t know if I’ve ruined things. I’m afraid I have.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood felt okay for several days, but your memory was still this bad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. The memory part—or thinking part, I suppose—has just been bad enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do in my sleep, and then the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll look at the page like… what was I trying to say?\n\nWhether my mood was okay for several days at the same time, I don’t know. I get caught on the fact that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I haven’t even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, and that’s usually the sort of thing I’d drift toward without thinking.\n\nI was supposed to help with choir planning too, and chess—well, I’ve just skipped it. And fishing, even that sounds like… more work than it’s worth, which is strange for me. I used to think about the tackle box, the little repairs, all of that. Now it just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever forget where you are, even in a place you know well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like suddenly looking around and not knowing where I am.\n\nI mean, I can walk into the garage and forget why I went in there, and then I notice the old paint cans still need sorting, and then I start thinking about whether I ever labeled the shelves properly—sorry, that’s not exactly it.\n\nIn places I know well, I don’t think I’ve forgotten where I am. It’s more that I lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Before you started feeling depressed, were your memory problems already there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know where to draw the line with “before,” honestly. What I can say is, the reason I came in is that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to do without even thinking—and then the numbers and words just… scatter. I end up staring at it, and it’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to do it. Whether that started first or the low mood did, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If someone gives you a hint, does it usually help you remember what you were trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes a hint helps a little, but not reliably. The reason I came in is more that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do in my sleep—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And then—well, I spend time out near the workshop, or I used to, anyway, with all the tools and the little projects piled up… I start getting scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone there too long.\n\nI don’t like saying that. I know that wasn’t exactly what you asked, or maybe it was. But yes, I feel like a burden.\n\nDoctor: When your mood briefly feels a little lighter, does your memory get better?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if it gets better just because my mood lifts for a minute. Maybe a little, maybe not.\n\nThe reason I came in is really that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll have the pen in my hand and then it’s like everything slips loose.\n\nEven on a lighter moment, if there is one, I can’t say it suddenly clears up the way I’d hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you know what month it is right now?\n\nPatient: The month… I should know that. I want to say it’s—see, this is exactly the kind of thing that’s been happening.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do without even thinking, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Like even a simple question, and I’m reaching for it and it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your family would truly be better off if you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I hate that word, dead. I don’t know if I believe it the way you’d believe something sensible, but the thought keeps coming up that they’d be better off without me.\n\nAnd when I’m out near the workshop—there are all those tools, old projects I never finished, things from when I used to keep busy—I get frightened of what I might start thinking if I stayed there alone too long. So yes… that thought is there, and it scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel certain that you have ruined things for your family, even when others tell you that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say certain. That sounds so… fixed, like a stamp on a document.\n\nI do worry I’ve made everything harder for them. I turn that over and over, and then someone will say, “No, that’s not true,” and for a little while I try to believe them. But it doesn’t quite stay put.\n\nIt’s like when I was sorting old papers in the garage—no, that’s not really what you asked. I’m sorry. I just mean… I’m not sure I can trust my own judgment about it. I don’t know if I’ve ruined things. I’m afraid I have.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood felt okay for several days, but your memory was still this bad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. The memory part—or thinking part, I suppose—has just been bad enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do in my sleep, and then the numbers and words just scatter on me. I’ll look at the page like… what was I trying to say?\n\nWhether my mood was okay for several days at the same time, I don’t know. I get caught on the fact that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I haven’t even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, and that’s usually the sort of thing I’d drift toward without thinking.\n\nI was supposed to help with choir planning too, and chess—well, I’ve just skipped it. And fishing, even that sounds like… more work than it’s worth, which is strange for me. I used to think about the tackle box, the little repairs, all of that. Now it just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever forget where you are, even in a place you know well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like suddenly looking around and not knowing where I am.\n\nI mean, I can walk into the garage and forget why I went in there, and then I notice the old paint cans still need sorting, and then I start thinking about whether I ever labeled the shelves properly—sorry, that’s not exactly it.\n\nIn places I know well, I don’t think I’ve forgotten where I am. It’s more that I lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Before you started feeling depressed, were your memory problems already there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know where to draw the line with “before,” honestly. What I can say is, the reason I came in is that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to do without even thinking—and then the numbers and words just… scatter. I end up staring at it, and it’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to do it. Whether that started first or the low mood did, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If someone gives you a hint, does it usually help you remember what you were trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes a hint helps a little, but not reliably. The reason I came in is more that my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do in my sleep—and the numbers and words just scatter on me. Someone can say, “You were going to mention the schedule,” and I’ll think, right, the schedule… but then I’m already losing the next piece of it. It’s like trying to keep sawdust in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble naming familiar objects or people?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a clear way. Sometimes a word sits there just out of reach, like I’ll point at the… you know, the thing on the counter, but I think everybody does that some.\n\nPeople’s names—well, I’ve never been great with names. At the hardware store I used to know half the fellows by sight, but names would float off unless I’d seen them a dozen times. That’s always been sort of true.\n\nSo, familiar objects or people? I haven’t really noticed that as a big problem. Not like looking at a chair and not knowing it’s a chair.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the month, do you often lose track of what day it is?\n\nPatient: The day, yeah… I lose the thread of it sometimes, but what really got me here is that my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—just a plain little note, nothing complicated—and before, I could do that practically in my sleep. Now the numbers and the words scatter on me, and I end up sitting there feeling foolish. The day of the week sort of gets mixed into that, I guess, but it’s more like everything slips away when I try to hold it still.\n\nDoctor: Can you still manage your own bills and money by yourself?\n\nPatient: The bills… my wife has been doing more of that lately. I used to keep a pretty good handle on things, or at least I thought I did.\n\nIt’s not just the money, though. I’ve let a lot slip. Church things I said I’d take care of, the garden, the youth maker program… that one bothers me. I loved being out in the workshop with the kids, showing them how to measure twice and keep their fingers clear, and now my wife and the pastor have had to step in because I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself. So the bills are kind of part of a bigger mess, I guess."
